=== BUGGY CODE ===
class Solution:
    def numPermsDISequence(self, s: str) -> int:
        mem=defaultdict(int)
        def dfs(i,val=0):
            if i==len(s):
                return 1
            if (i,val) in mem:
                return mem[i,val]
            p=0
            if s[i]=="D":
                for ind in range(0,val+1):
                    p+=dfs(i+1,ind)%(10**9+7)
            else:
                for ind in range(val+1,i+1):
                    p+=dfs(i+1,ind)%(10**9+7)
            mem[i,val]=p
            return p
        return dfs(0)

Observe the following faulty python code. It contains one or more bugs.
Fix the code and output only the fixed code.

```python
class Solution:
    def numPermsDISequence(self, s: str) -> int:
        mem=defaultdict(int)
        def dfs(i,val=0):
            if i==len(s):
                return 1
            if (i,val) in mem:
                return mem[i,val]
            p=0
            if s[i]=="D":
                for ind in range(0,val+1):
                    p+=dfs(i+1,ind)%(10**9+7)
            else:
                for ind in range(val+1,i+1):
                    p+=dfs(i+1,ind)%(10**9+7)
            mem[i,val]=p
            return p
        return dfs(0)
```

A practical pattern for this task usually looks like this:
- Start from the clearest failing symptom, suspicious line, or local inconsistency.
- Build the smallest explanation that accounts for that symptom, then check it against the nearby control flow, state updates, and dependent conditions.
- Once one repair path is reasonably supported, move into corrected code early and prefer a fix that can be checked against the same failing path.
- After the edit, rerun that path or the most relevant edge case.
- Then do one small coupled-region scan: a directly connected guard, index update, return path, or call site that could still break the same fix.
- If the recheck is still off, refine the same local region before opening a distant new theory.
- Once the failing path and its directly coupled region look consistent, finalize the code.

Known recurrence for "Permutations of length n+1 with DI" often:
dp[i][j] = number of permutations of length i+1 ending with j (0<=j<=i).
To compute dp[i][j] from dp[i-1][k], we consider adding j as last element to a permutation of length i. Since the new element j is not necessarily largest; to map to previous permutation of length i-1, we need adjust ranks. The recurrence:
If s[i-1] == 'I': dp[i][j] = sum_{k=0}^{j-1} dp[i-1][k]
If s[i-1] == 'D': dp[i][j] = sum_{k=j}^{i-1} dp[i-1][k]
This is standard.

class Solution:
    def numPermsDISequence(self, s: str) -> int:
        n = len(s)
        @lru_cache(None)
        def dp(i, j):
            if i == n:

            if s[i] == 'D':
                return sum(dp(i+1, k) for k in range(j))

def dfs(i,val):
    if i == n: return 1
    if (i,val) in mem: return mem[i,val]
    p=0
    if s[i]=='D':
        for ind in range(0, val+1):
            p = (p + dfs(i+1, ind)) % MOD
    else:
        for ind in range(val+1, i+2):
            p = (p + dfs(i+1, ind)) % MOD
    mem[i,val]=p
    return p
return dfs(0)

class Solution:
    def numPermsDISequence(self, s: str) -> int:
        MOD = 10**9 + 7
        n = len(s)
        mem = {}

        def dfs(i: int, val: int) -> int:
            if i == n:
                return 1
            key = (i, val)
            if key in mem:
                return mem[key]

            total = 0
            if s[i] == 'D':
                for nxt in range(val + 1):
                    total = (total + dfs(i + 1, nxt)) % MOD
            else:
                for nxt in range(val + 1, i + 2):
                    total = (total + dfs(i + 1, nxt)) % MOD

            mem[key] = total
            return total

        return dfs(0, 0)

Need check `range(val + 1)` for D: if val=0, range(1) includes 0. Good. If val could be -? no.

Potential issue: For I, `range(val + 1, i + 2)` upper exclusive is i+2, so includes i+1. Good.

```python
class Solution:
    def numPermsDISequence(self, s: str) -> int:
        MOD = 10**9 + 7
        n = len(s)
        mem = {}

        def dfs(i: int, val: int) -> int:
            if i == n:
                return 1
            key = (i, val)
            if key in mem:
                return mem[key]

            total = 0
            if s[i] == 'D':
                for nxt in range(val + 1):
                    total = (total + dfs(i + 1, nxt)) % MOD
            else:
                for nxt in range(val + 1, i + 2):
                    total = (total + dfs(i + 1, nxt)) % MOD

            mem[key] = total
            return total

        return dfs(0, 0)
```

    def numPermsDISequence(self, s: str) -> int:
        MOD = 10**9 + 7

Let's maybe think about if there is a more direct fix preserving `defaultdict`:
```
from collections import defaultdict
class Solution:
    def numPermsDISequence(self, s: str) -> int:
        MOD=10**9+7
        mem=defaultdict(int)
        def dfs(i,val=0):
            if i==len(s): return 1
            if (i,val) in mem: return mem[i,val]
            p=0
            if s[i]=="D":
                for ind in range(0,val+1):
                    p=(p+dfs(i+1,ind))%MOD
            else:
                for ind in range(val+1,i+2):
                    p=(p+dfs(i+1,ind))%MOD
            mem[i,val]=p
            return p
        return dfs(0)
```
This is closer to original. But our dict version is also fine. Which is preferable? The original uses `defaultdict`, but if we include import, it's self-contained. Using dict avoids import. Both accepted. The prompt says "Fix the code" not "minimize changes". I'd output clean code with dict.

Let's write:

class Solution:
    def numPermsDISequence(self, s: str) -> int:
        MOD = 10**9 + 7
        n = len(s)
        mem = {}

        def dfs(i, val):
            if i == n:
                return 1
            key = (i, val)
            if key in mem:
                return mem[key]

            total = 0
            if s[i] == 'D':
                for nxt in range(val + 1):
                    total = (total + dfs(i + 1, nxt)) % MOD
            else:
                for nxt in range(val + 1, i + 2):
                    total = (total + dfs(i + 1, nxt)) % MOD

            mem[key] = total
            return total

        return dfs(0, 0)

This is clean.

So for a fixed previous rank k, the new rank j can be any from 0 to k. Thus D transition from current k to next j includes j=k. So range 0..k. Good.

=== RAW ANSWER ===
```python
class Solution:
    def numPermsDISequence(self, s: str) -> int:
        MOD = 10**9 + 7
        n = len(s)
        mem = {}

        def dfs(i, val):
            if i == n:
                return 1
            key = (i, val)
            if key in mem:
                return mem[key]

            total = 0
            if s[i] == 'D':
                for nxt in range(val + 1):
                    total = (total + dfs(i + 1, nxt)) % MOD
            else:
                for nxt in range(val + 1, i + 2):
                    total = (total + dfs(i + 1, nxt)) % MOD

            mem[key] = total
            return total

        return dfs(0, 0)
```

=== FIXED CODE ===
class Solution:
    def numPermsDISequence(self, s: str) -> int:
        MOD = 10**9 + 7
        n = len(s)
        mem = {}

        def dfs(i, val):
            if i == n:
                return 1
            key = (i, val)
            if key in mem:
                return mem[key]

            total = 0
            if s[i] == 'D':
                for nxt in range(val + 1):
                    total = (total + dfs(i + 1, nxt)) % MOD
            else:
                for nxt in range(val + 1, i + 2):
                    total = (total + dfs(i + 1, nxt)) % MOD

            mem[key] = total
            return total

        return dfs(0, 0)
